 The spiders  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  are  e e -positive Thanks:  David Wang is the corresponding author, and is supported by the General Program of National Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant No. 12171034).  Abstract. 
    
 By using the composition method, we establish the  e e -positivity of spiders of the form  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) , which was conjectured by Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang. Following the divide-and-conquer strategy, we group one or two  e J e_{J} -terms that have positive coefficients with each  e I e_{I} -term that has a negative coefficient, where the compositions  J J  are selected to be obtained by rearranging the parts of  I I , and show the positivity of the sum of those coefficients. Our main contribution is an explicit construction of the injection. 
  
 
 
 Key words and phrases:  chromatic symmetric function;  e e -positivity; Stanley and Stembridge’s conjecture; the composition method
 
 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification 05E05 05C15 05A20

 References 
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 In his study of the chromatic polynomial,  Stanley [6, Section 5]  introduced the concept of chromatic symmetric functions. Given a basis  b λ b_{\lambda}  for symmetric functions, a symmetric function  f f  is said to be  b b -positive if every  b b -coefficient is nonnegative.
 Stanley [6, Section 5]  asked a question: Which graphs are  e e -positive?
He also conjectured that all incomparability graphs of  ( 3 + 1 ) (3+1) -free posets are  e e -positive, which was confirmed by  Hikita [4] .
 Tom and Vailaya [9]  established
the  e e -positivity of the sum graph of natural unit interval graphs and cycles,
which generalize hat-chains  [ 12 ] .
They also confirmed the  e e -positivity of noncrossing cycle-chord graphs,
which generalize cycle-chords  [ 10 ] .
Very recently,
 Chen et al. [1]  showed the  e e -positivity of clocks,
which do not belong to any graph class above.
The general question of which graphs are  e e -positive is still open. 
 
 
 A parallel study is the non- e e -positivity of graphs.
 Dahlberg et al. [3]  conjectured that the maximum degree of any  e e -positive tree is  3 3 ,
which was attacked by  Tom [8]  by proving that
this maximum degree is at most  4 4 . 
 
 
 A particular class of trees, the spiders,
plays an essential role along this study.
For any partition
 λ = λ 1 ​ ⋯ ​ λ d \lambda=\lambda_{1}\dotsm\lambda_{d}  of  n − 1 n-1 
with  d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 ,
the  spider   S ⁡ ( λ ) S(\lambda)  is the  n n -vertex tree consisting
of paths of lengths  λ 1 , … , λ d \lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}  with a common end.
For instance,
the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is obtained by adding a pendent vertex to the path of order  6 ​ m + 3 6m+3 , see  Fig.   1.1 . 
 
 length  4 ​ m + 2 4m+2 length  2 ​ m 2m 
 Figure 1.1.  The spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) . 
 
 
 Dahlberg et al. [3, Lemma 13]  showed that
if a connected graph  G G  has a connected partition of type  λ \lambda ,
then so does the spider  S ⁡ ( λ ) S(\lambda) ,
where  λ \lambda  is the partition consisting
of the sizes of connected components that are obtained by removing a vertex of degree at least 3 from  G G .
Therefore, the  e e -positivity of a tree implies
the  e e -positivity of certain spider
by virtue of  Wolfgang III ’s criterion,
which states that
any connected  e e -positive graph has a connected partition of every type. 
 
 
 Wang and Wang [11]  conjectured that a spider  S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) S(a,\,b,\,c)  with the shortest leg length at least  3 3  is  e e -positive
if and only if it is  S ⁡ ( 8 , 5 , 3 ) S(8,5,3)  or  S ⁡ ( 14 , 9 , 5 ) S(14,9,5) .
In contrast, in view of their results on the  e e -positivity of spiders  S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) S(a,\,b,\,c)  with  c ∈ { 1 , 2 } c\in\{1,2\} , it is quite unpredictable that such a spider
is  e e -positive or not. Among this disorder,
however,  Zheng [14, Conjecture 6.2]  presented  Conjecture   1.1 
and attributed it to Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang. 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.1   (Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang) . 
 
 For any integer  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 ,
the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 For example, the  e e -positivity of  S ⁡ ( 6 ,  2 ,  1 ) S(6,\,2,\,1)  is clear from the  e e -expansion 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( 6 , 2 , 1 ) \displaystyle X_{S(6,2,1)} 
 = 10 ​ e 10 + 17 ​ e 91 + 22 ​ e 82 + 7 ​ e 81 2 + 11 ​ e 73 + 24 ​ e 721 + 38 ​ e 64 + 32 ​ e 631 + 26 ​ e 62 2 + 5 ​ e 621 2 \displaystyle=10e_{10}+17e_{91}+22e_{82}+7e_{81^{2}}+11e_{73}+24e_{721}+38e_{64}+32e_{631}+26e_{62^{2}}+5e_{621^{2}} 
 
 
 
 
 + 20 ​ e 5 2 + 55 ​ e 541 + 37 ​ e 532 + 16 ​ e 531 2 + 20 ​ e 52 2 ​ 1 + 42 ​ e 4 2 ​ 2 + 9 ​ e 4 2 ​ 1 2 + e 43 2 + 59 ​ e 4321 \displaystyle\quad+20e_{5^{2}}+55e_{541}+37e_{532}+16e_{531^{2}}+20e_{52^{2}1}+42e_{4^{2}2}+9e_{4^{2}1^{2}}+e_{43^{2}}+59e_{4321} 
 
 
 
 
 + 22 ​ e 42 3 + 3 ​ e 42 2 ​ 1 2 + 8 ​ e 3 3 ​ 1 + 9 ​ e 3 2 ​ 2 2 + 8 ​ e 3 2 ​ 21 2 + 9 ​ e 32 3 ​ 1 + 2 ​ e 2 5 . \displaystyle\quad+22e_{42^{3}}+3e_{42^{2}1^{2}}+8e_{3^{3}1}+9e_{3^{2}2^{2}}+8e_{3^{2}21^{2}}+9e_{32^{3}1}+2e_{2^{5}}. 
 
 
 
 
 The following more intriguing conjecture can be found from  Zheng [14, Conjecture 6.3] . 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.2   (Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang) . 
 
 For any integers  m , n ≥ 1 m,n\geq 1 ,
the spider  S ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( n ! ​ m + 1 ) , n ! ​ m ,  1 ) S(n(n!m+1),\,n!m,\,1)  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 It is true for  n = 1 n=1 , since 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( m + 1 , m ,  1 ) = 1 2 ​ ( X B ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) + e 1 ​ X P 2 ​ m + 2 ) , X_{S(m+1,\,m,\,1)}=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{\big(}{X_{B_{(m+1)(m+1)}}+e_{1}X_{P_{2m+2}}}\mathclose{\big)}, 
 
 
 where  B ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) B_{(m+1)(m+1)}  is the generalized bull graph obtained by adding a path of length  m m 
to each of two distinct vertices of a triangle, which is  e e -positive.
See  Dahlberg et al. [2, Page 2681] .
For  n = 2 n=2 ,  Conjecture   1.2  reduces to  Conjecture   1.1 . 
 
 
 This paper is devoted to the confirmation of  Conjecture   1.1  by giving an explicit injection from negative terms to the positive. In  Section   2 , we give a quick view of necessary notion and notations,
as well as two preliminary lemmas.
Our proof starts from  Section   3 . 
 
 
 
 2.  Preliminary 

 
 Let  n n  be a positive integer.
A  composition  of  n n  is a sequence of positive integers that sum to  n n , commonly denoted
 I = i 1 ⋯ i l ⊨ n I=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}\vDash n .
We denote  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | I \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = n \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{I}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=n  and  ℓ ⁡ ( I ) = l \ell(I)=l .
The integers  i k i_{k}  are called  parts  of  I I ,
and we denote the  j j th last part  i ℓ ⁡ ( I ) + 1 − j i_{\ell(I)+1-j}  by  i − j i_{-j} 
for notational convenience.
Whenever a capital letter such like  I , J ​ … I,J\dots 
is adopted to denote a composition,
we use the corresponding small letter counterparts  i , j ​ … i,j\dots 
with integer subscripts to denote the parts.
Following  Wang and Zhou [12] , the  a a -surplus  of  I I  is the number 
 

 
 
 Θ I ( a ) = min { i 1 + ⋯ + i k : 0 ≤ k ≤ ℓ ( I ) , i 1 + ⋯ + i k ≥ a } − a . \Theta_{I}(a)=\min\{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\colon 0\leq k\leq\ell(I),\ i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}\geq a\}-a. 
 
 
 It is clear that  Θ I ​ ( a ) ≥ 0 \Theta_{I}(a)\geq 0 . 
 
 
 A  partition  of  n n  is a composition of  n n 
in non-increasing order, commonly denoted as 
 

 
 
 λ = λ 1 λ 2 ⋯ ⊢ n , \lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\vdash n, 
 
 
 where  λ 1 ≥ λ 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 1 \lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 1 .
A  symmetric function of homogeneous degree  n n  over a commutative ring  R R  with identity  is a formal power series 
 

 
 
 f ( x 1 , x 2 , … ) = ∑ λ = λ 1 λ 2 ⋯ ⊢ n c λ x 1 λ 1 x 2 λ 2 ⋯ , where  c λ ∈ R , f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)=\sum_{\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\vdash n}c_{\lambda}x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots,\quad\text{where $c_{\lambda}\in R$}, 
 
 
 such that  f ⁡ ( x 1 , x 2 , … ) = f ⁡ ( x σ ⁡ ( 1 ) , x σ ⁡ ( 2 ) , … ) f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)=f(x_{\sigma(1)},x_{\sigma(2)},\dots)  for any permutation  σ \sigma .
For an introduction on symmetric functions, see  Stanley [7, Chapter 7] .
For any partition  λ = λ 1 ​ λ 2 ​ ⋯ \lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\dotsm ,
the  elementary symmetric function  with respect to  λ \lambda 
is  e λ = e λ 1 e λ 2 ⋯ e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda_{1}}e_{\lambda_{2}}\cdots ,
where  e r e_{r}  is the sum of all products of  r r  distinct variables  x i x_{i} .
Elementary symmetric functions form a basis of the algebra of symmetric functions.
A symmetric function is  e e -positive  if all its  e e -coefficients are nonnegative. We will use the extended symmetric function  e I e_{I}  with composition indices  I I , which is defined as  e I = e λ e_{I}=e_{\lambda} , where  λ \lambda  is the underlying partition whose parts
are those of  I I .
For a symmetric function  f f , the expression 
 

 
 
 f = ∑ I ⊨ n c I ​ e I f=\sum_{I\vDash n}c_{I}e_{I} 
 
 
 is called an  e I e_{I} -expansion  of  f f .
An  e I e_{I} -expansion is positive if  c I ≥ 0 c_{I}\geq 0  for all  I I . Any symmetric function   f f  having a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 Stanley [6]  introduced the concept of the chromatic symmetric function of a graph  G G  to be the symmetric function 
 

 
 
 X G = ∑ κ x κ ⁡ ( v 1 ) ​ ⋯ ​ x κ ⁡ ( v n ) , X_{G}=\sum_{\kappa}x_{\kappa(v_{1})}\dotsm x_{\kappa(v_{n})}, 
 
 
 where  κ \kappa  runs over proper colorings of  G G ,
and  v 1 , … , v n v_{1},\dots,v_{n}  are the vertices of  G G .
For instance, the complete graph  K n K_{n} 
has chromatic symmetric function  X K n = n ! ​ e n X_{K_{n}}=n!e_{n} . 
 
 
 For convenience, we introduce the functions  w I ′ w_{I}^{\prime}  and  w I w_{I}  defined for compositions  I = i 1 ​ i 2 ​ ⋯ I=i_{1}i_{2}\dotsm  by 
 

 
 
 w I ′ = ( i 2 − 1 ) ​ ( i 3 − 1 ) ​ ⋯ and w I = i 1 ​ w I ′ . w_{I}^{\prime}=(i_{2}-1)(i_{3}-1)\dotsm\quad\text{and}\quad w_{I}=i_{1}w_{I}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Denote by  P n P_{n}  the  n n -vertex path.
 Shareshian and Wachs [5, Table 1]  obtained the following formula. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.1   ( Shareshian and Wachs ) . 
 
 X P n = ∑ I ⊨ n w I ​ e I X_{P_{n}}=\sum_{I\vDash n}w_{I}e_{I} . 
 
 
 
 For instance, 
 

 
 
 X P 3 = w 3 ​ e 3 + w 21 ​ e 21 + w 12 ​ e 12 + w 111 ​ e 111 = 3 ​ e 3 + e 12 . X_{P_{3}}=w_{3}e_{3}+w_{21}e_{21}+w_{12}e_{12}+w_{111}e_{111}=3e_{3}+e_{12}. 
 
 
 
 
 Zheng [14, Lemma 4.4]  presented the following formula for the chromatic symmetric functions of spiders with  3 3  legs. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.2   ( Zheng ) . 
 
 For any partition  ( a , b , c ) ⊢ n − 1 (a,b,c)\vdash n-1 , 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) = X P n + ∑ i = 1 c ( X P i ​ X P n − i − X P b + i ​ X P n − b − i ) . X_{S(a,b,c)}=X_{P_{n}}+\sum_{i=1}^{c}(X_{P_{i}}X_{P_{n-i}}-X_{P_{b+i}}X_{P_{n-b-i}}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Confirming the  e e -positivity of spiders  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) 

 
 Let  n = 6 ​ m + 4 n=6m+4 .
Our goal is to find a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion of  X S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) X_{S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)} . First, we derive an  e I e_{I} -expansion for  X S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) X_{S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)} .
Let
 𝒞 n = { I ⊨ n : i 1 , i 2 , ⋯ ≥ 2 } \mathcal{C}_{n}=\{I\vDash n\colon i_{1},i_{2},\dots\geq 2\} . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 For any  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) \displaystyle X_{S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)} 
 = e 1 2 ​ ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 , Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I + e 1 ​ X 1 + X 0 , \displaystyle=e_{1}^{2}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2},\ \Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1}w_{1I}e_{I}+e_{1}X_{1}+X_{0}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 X 1 \displaystyle X_{1} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 2 w I ​ e I + ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I + ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 ( k 1 ​ j 1 − 2 ​ j 1 + 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ e J ​ K , and \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 2\end{subarray}}w_{I}e_{I}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1\end{subarray}}w_{1I}e_{I}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2}\\
K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}\end{subarray}}(k_{1}j_{1}-2j_{1}+1)w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}e_{J\!K},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 (3.1) 
 
 X 0 \displaystyle X_{0} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n w I ​ e I − ∑ P ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 3 , Q ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 w P ​ w Q ​ e P ​ Q . \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}w_{I}e_{I}-\sum_{P\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+3},\ Q\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}}w_{P}w_{Q}e_{PQ}. 
 
 
 As a consequence, the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is  e e -positive if and only if  X 0 X_{0}  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  G = S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) G=S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) . By  Lemmas   2.2  and  2.1 , 
 

 
 
 X G \displaystyle X_{G} 
 = X P n + X P 1 ​ X P n − 1 − X P 2 ​ m + 1 ​ X P 4 ​ m + 3 = ∑ I ⊨ n w I ​ e I + ∑ I ⊨ n − 1 w I ​ e 1 ​ I − ∑ J ⊨ 4 ​ m + 3 , K ⊨ 2 ​ m + 1 w J ​ w K ​ e J ​ K . \displaystyle=X_{P_{n}}+X_{P_{1}}X_{P_{n-1}}-X_{P_{2m+1}}X_{P_{4m+3}}=\sum_{I\vDash n}w_{I}e_{I}+\sum_{I\vDash n-1}w_{I}e_{1I}-\sum_{J\vDash 4m+3,\ K\vDash 2m+1}w_{J}w_{K}e_{J\!K}. 
 
 
 Arranging the terms according to the number of parts  1 1  in compositions,
we obtain 
 

 
 
 X G = X 0 + e 1 ​ Y 1 + e 1 2 ​ Y 2 , X_{G}=X_{0}+e_{1}Y_{1}+e_{1}^{2}Y_{2}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 Y 1 \displaystyle Y_{1} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 ( w 1 ​ I + w I ) ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 w 1 ​ J ​ w K ​ e J ​ K − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 3 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m w J ​ w 1 ​ K ​ e J ​ K , and \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}}(w_{1I}+w_{I})e_{I}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}}w_{1J}w_{K}e_{J\!K}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+3},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m}}w_{J}w_{1K}e_{J\!K},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 Y 2 \displaystyle Y_{2} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 w 1 ​ I ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m w 1 ​ J ​ w 1 ​ K ​ e J ​ K = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 , Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I . \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2}}w_{1I}e_{I}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m}}w_{1J}w_{1K}e_{J\!K}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2},\,\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1}w_{1I}e_{I}. 
 
 
 We then regroup the terms in  Eq.   3.2 
according to the value of  Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) \Theta_{I}(4m+2)  as 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 Y 1 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 , Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 2 ( w 1 ​ I + w I ) ​ e I + Z 1 + Z 0 , Y_{1}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1},\ \Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 2}(w_{1I}+w_{I})e_{I}+Z_{1}+Z_{0}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 Z 1 \displaystyle Z_{1} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) = 1 ( w 1 ​ I + w I ) ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 3 K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m w J ​ w 1 ​ K ​ e J ​ K = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) = 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I , and \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)=1\end{subarray}}(w_{1I}+w_{I})e_{I}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+3}\\
K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m}\end{subarray}}w_{J}w_{1K}e_{J\!K}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)=1\end{subarray}}w_{1I}e_{I},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 (3.5) 
 
 Z 0 \displaystyle Z_{0} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) = 0 ( w 1 ​ I + w I ) ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 w 1 ​ J ​ w K ​ e J ​ K = ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 ( w 1 ​ J ​ K + w J ​ K − w 1 ​ J ​ w K ) ​ e J ​ K , \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)=0\end{subarray}}(w_{1I}+w_{I})e_{I}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2}\\
K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}\end{subarray}}w_{1J}w_{K}e_{J\!K}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2}\\
K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}\end{subarray}}(w_{1J\!K}+w_{J\!K}-w_{1J}w_{K})e_{J\!K}, 
 
 
 in which the last coefficient can be simplified as 
 

 
 
 w 1 ​ J ​ K + w J ​ K − w 1 ​ J ​ w K = ( k 1 ​ j 1 − 2 ​ j 1 + 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ . w_{1J\!K}+w_{J\!K}-w_{1J}w_{K}=(k_{1}j_{1}-2j_{1}+1)w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Substituting  Eqs.   3.4  and  3.5  into  Eq.   3.3 ,
we can recast  Y 1 Y_{1}  as the desired symmetric function   X 1 X_{1} .
Note that 
 

 
 
 k 1 ​ j 1 − 2 ​ j 1 + 1 ≥ 1 , k_{1}j_{1}-2j_{1}+1\geq 1, 
 
 
 since  j 1 , k 1 ≥ 2 j_{1},k_{1}\geq 2 . Thus the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is  e e -positive if and only if  X 0 X_{0}  is  e e -positive.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now for any term  w P ​ w Q ​ e P ​ Q w_{P}w_{Q}e_{PQ}  in  X 0 X_{0} , it is natural to choose  w P ​ Q ​ e P ​ Q w_{PQ}e_{PQ}  and  w Q ​ P ​ e Q ​ P w_{QP}e_{QP}  from the positive sum  ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n w I ​ e I \sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}w_{I}e_{I}  and show that 
 

 
 
 w P ​ Q + w Q ​ P − w P ​ w Q ≥ 0 . w_{PQ}+w_{QP}-w_{P}w_{Q}\geq 0. 
 
 
 But the composition  P ​ Q PQ  (or  Q ​ P QP , or both) might be used more than once. In  Lemma   3.2 , we handle the pairs  ( P , Q ) (P,Q)  such that each of the compositions  P ​ Q PQ  and  Q ​ P QP  is used only once in the positive sum, and characterize the remaining part in  X 0 X_{0} . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.2 .

 (3.18)

 f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) ≥ 0 unless  ( j , k , l ) = ( 2 , 3 , 3 )  or  min ⁡ { k , l } = 2 . f(j,k,l)\geq 0\quad\text{unless $(j,k,l)=(2,3,3)$ or $\min\{k,\,l\}=2$}.

when  k + l ≥ 6 k+l\geq 6 . This proves the claim, and  Eq.   3.18  follows.

 It is direct to see that  e φ 1 ​ ( J , K , L ) = e J ​ K ​ L e_{\varphi_{1}(J,K,L)}=e_{J\!K\!L} . On the
other hand,
since  f ⁡ ( 2 , 3 , 3 ) = − 2 f(2,3,3)=-2 , 
 

 
 
 c 1 ​ ( J , K , L ) = { 2 ​ ( 3 − 1 ) ​ ( 3 − 1 ) − 2 = 6 , if  j − 1 = 2 3 ​ ( 2 − 1 ) ​ ( 3 − 1 ) − 2 = 4 , if  j − 1 ≥ 3 c_{1}(J,K,L)=\begin{cases}2(3-1)(3-1)-2=6,&if $j_{-1}=2$\\

 is positive. For example, 
 

 
 
 φ 1 ​ ( 26 ,  34 ,  322 ) = 3634222 and φ 1 ​ ( 242 ,  34 ,  322 ) = 24334222 . \varphi_{1}(26,\,34,\,322)=3634222\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{1}(242,\,34,\,322)=24334222.

 (2)  
 
 The map  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  is injective. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 1 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{1} .
Recall that 
 

 
 
 𝐓 1 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 3 , k 1 = 3 , j 1 = 2 , K ≥ L } . \mathbf{T}_{1}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=3,\,k_{1}=3,\,j_{1}=2,\,K\geq L\}. 
 
 
 Let  I = φ 1 ​ ( J , K , L ) = P ​ K ​ R I=\varphi_{1}(J,K,L)=P\!K\!R .
First of all, we check  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} .
Recall that 
 

 
 
 𝒜 = { I ∈ 𝒞 n : Θ I ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) ​ Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) ≠ 0 } . \mathcal{A}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\colon\Theta_{I}(2m+1)\Theta_{I}(4m+3)\neq 0\}. 
 
 
 In fact, we have
 Θ I ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) = 2 \Theta_{I}(2m+1)=2 ,
since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | − j 1 + l 1 = 2 ​ m + 3 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}-j_{1}+l_{1}=2m+3  and since  p − 1 ≥ l 1 = 3 p_{-1}\geq l_{1}=3  by definition.
On the other hand, we have  Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) = 1 \Theta_{I}(4m+3)=1 ,
since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | K \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 4 ​ m + 4 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{K}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=4m+4  and since  k − 1 ≥ 2 k_{-1}\geq 2 .
This proves  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} .
It follows that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m 
and  I ∈ 𝒮 1 I\in\mathcal{S}_{1} . This proves the first statement  Item   1 . 
 
 
 Next, we shall show that  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  is injective.
Suppose that  ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 1 (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{1}  and 
 

 
 
 P ​ K ​ R = φ 1 ​ ( J , K , L ) = φ 1 ​ ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) = P ′ ​ K ′ ​ R ′ . P\!K\!R=\varphi_{1}(J,K,L)=\varphi_{1}(J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=P^{\prime}\!K^{\prime}\!R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Let  I = P ​ K ​ R I=P\!K\!R .
Since each of  P P  and  P ′ P^{\prime}  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 3 2m+3 , we deduce that  P = P ′ P=P^{\prime} .
Similarly, we deduce that  K = K ′ K=K^{\prime}  and  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} .
Since  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} ) can be obtained from  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} )
by replacing the first part with  3 3 , we find  L = L ′ L=L^{\prime} .
Since  P = P ′ P=P^{\prime} , we find  ℓ ⁡ ( J ) = ℓ ⁡ ( J ′ ) \ell(J)=\ell(J^{\prime}) ,
and  j s = j s ′ j_{s}=j_{s}^{\prime}  for  2 ≤ s ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( J ) − 1 2\leq s\leq\ell(J)-1 ; moreover, 
 

 
 
 min ⁡ { j − 1 ,  3 } = min ⁡ { j − 1 ′ ,  3 } and max ⁡ { j − 1 ,  3 } = max ⁡ { j − 1 ′ ,  3 } . \min\{j_{-1},\,3\}=\min\{j^{\prime}_{-1},\,3\}\quad\text{and}\quad\max\{j_{-1},\,3\}=\max\{j^{\prime}_{-1},\,3\}. 
 
 
 It follows that  j − 1 = j − 1 ′ j_{-1}=j_{-1}^{\prime} .
Since  j 1 = 2 = j 1 ′ j_{1}=2=j_{1}^{\prime} , we find  J = J ′ J=J^{\prime} .
This proves the second statement  Item   2  and completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Secondly, let us define  φ 2 \varphi_{2} .
For any 
 

 
 
 ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 2 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , f ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) < 0 , k 1  is even,  k 1 ≥ 4 } , (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{2}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,f(j_{1},k_{1},2)<0,\,\text{$k_{1}$ is even, $k_{1}\geq 4$}\}, 
 
 
 define  φ 2 : 𝐓 2 → 𝒜 \varphi_{2}\colon\mathbf{T}_{2}\to\mathcal{A} 
by 
 

 
 (3.22) 
 
 φ 2 ( J , K , L ) = J Q R , where  Q = 2 ​ k 2 ​ ⋯ ​ k − 1 ​ k 1  and  R = l 2 ​ ⋯ ​ l − 1 . \varphi_{2}(J,K,L)=JQR,\quad\text{where
$Q=2k_{2}\dotsm k_{-1}k_{1}$ and
$R=l_{2}\dotsm l_{-1}$}. 
 
 
 In other words,

by exchanging  k 1 k_{1}  and  l 1 l_{1} ,
see  Fig.   3.2 . We will verify that  φ 2 ​ ( 𝐓 2 ) ⊆ 𝒜 \varphi_{2}(\mathbf{T}_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{A}  in

 J J k 1 k_{1} k 2 k_{2} … \dots k − 1 k_{-1} 2 2 l 2 l_{2} … \dots l − 1 l_{-1}

 It is direct to see that  e φ 2 ​ ( J , K , L ) = e J ​ K ​ L e_{\varphi_{2}(J,K,L)}=e_{J\!K\!L} . On the other hand, 
 

 
 (3.23) 
 
 c 2 ​ ( J , K , L ) = j 1 ​ ( k 1 − 1 ) ​ ( 2 − 1 ) + f ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) = ( j 1 − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k 1 − 5 ) − 1 ≥ 2 . c_{2}(J,K,L)=j_{1}(k_{1}-1)(2-1)+f(j_{1},k_{1},2)=(j_{1}-1)(2k_{1}-5)-1\geq 2. 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 For example,

 The map  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  is injective. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof.

 𝒜 = { I ∈ 𝒞 n : Θ I ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) ​ Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) ≠ 0 } . \mathcal{A}=\{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\colon\Theta_{I}(2m+1)\Theta_{I}(4m+3)\neq 0\}. 
 
 
 Now let us check  I ∈ 𝒜 ∩ 𝒮 2 I\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2} . In fact,

since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 2 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+2  and  j − 1 ≥ 2 j_{-1}\geq 2 .
On the other hand, we have
 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) = 2 \Theta_{I}(4m+3)=2 ,
since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 4 ​ m + 5 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=4m+5  and  q − 1 = k 1 ≥ 4 q_{-1}=k_{1}\geq 4 .
It follows that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 3 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+3  and  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m − 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m-1 .
Moreover,  q 1 = l 1 = 2 q_{1}=l_{1}=2 ,  q − 1 = k 1 q_{-1}=k_{1}  is even and is at least  4 4 .
This verifies  I ∈ 𝒜 ∩ 𝒮 2 I\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2} . 
 
 
 Next, we will show that  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  is injective.
Suppose that  ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 2 (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{2}  and 
 

 
 
 J ​ Q ​ R = φ 2 ​ ( J , K , L ) = φ 2 ​ ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) = J ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ . JQR=\varphi_{2}(J,K,L)=\varphi_{2}(J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=J^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Let  I = J ​ Q ​ R = J ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ I=JQR=J^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} .
Since  I ∈ 𝒮 2 I\in\mathcal{S}_{2} ,
the composition  J J  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 2 2m+2 ,
and so is  J ′ J^{\prime} . Thus  J = J ′ J=J^{\prime} .
For the same reason, we have  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime}  and  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} .
By definition, the composition  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} )
can be obtained by inserting a part  2 2 
at the beginning of  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ).
Since  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} , we find  L = L ′ L=L^{\prime} .
On the other hand,
the composition  K K  (resp.,  K ′ K^{\prime} )
can be obtained from  Q Q  (resp.,  Q ′ Q^{\prime} )
by removing the first part and then moving the last part to the beginning.
Since  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime} , we derive that  K = K ′ K=K^{\prime} .
This proves the injectivity of  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  and completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Thirdly, let us define  φ 3 \varphi_{3} .
For any triple 
 

 
 
 ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 3 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , f ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) < 0 , k 1  is odd } , (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{3}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,f(j_{1},k_{1},2)<0,\,\text{$k_{1}$ is odd}\}, 
 
 
 each of  K K  and  L L  contains at least one odd part since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | K \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | L \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{K}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{L}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1 .
We call a composition  even 
if it has only even parts.
For any composition   I I ,
if  I I  has at least one odd part,
let  f ⁡ ( I ) f\!(I)  (resp.,  l ​ o ​ ( I ) lo(I) )
be the index of the first (resp., last) odd part of  I I , and we use  I f ​ o I_{f\!o}  (resp.,  I l ​ o I_{lo} ) as a shorthand for  I f ⁡ ( I ) I_{f\!(I)}  (resp.,  I l ​ o ​ ( I ) I_{lo(I)} ), which is the first (resp., last) odd part of  I I .
Define  φ 3 : 𝐓 3 → 𝒜 \varphi_{3}\colon\mathbf{T}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}  by 
 

 
 (3.24) 
 
 φ 3 ​ ( J , K , L ) = J ​ Q ​ R , \varphi_{3}(J,K,L)=JQR, 
 
 
 where
 Q = k 2 ​ ⋯ ​ k − 1 Q=k_{2}\dotsm k_{-1}  is obtained from  K K  by removing the first part  k 1 k_{1} ,
and  R R  is obtained by inserting   k 1 k_{1}  into  L L  as: 
 

 
 (3.25) 
 
 R = 2 ​ l 2 ​ ⋯ ​ l min ⁡ { f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 } ⋅ k 1 ⋅ l min ⁡ { f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 } + 1 ​ ⋯ ​ l − 1 . R=2l_{2}\dotsm l_{\min\{f\!(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-lo(K)+1\}}\cdot k_{1}\cdot l_{\min\{f\!(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-lo(K)+1\}+1}\dotsm l_{-1}. 
 
 
 In  Eq.   3.25 , the value  f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 f\!(L)-1  is selected to ensure that  k 1 k_{1}  is the first odd part of  R R , and the value  ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 \ell(K)-lo(K)+1  is selected for proving  Eq.   3.32 , as
will be seen. In other words,
the composition  φ 3 ​ ( J , K , L ) \varphi_{3}(J,K,L)  is obtained in the following  2 2  steps: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Move the part  k 1 k_{1}  into  L L  between
the parts  l f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 l_{f\!(L)-1}  and  l f ​ o l_{f\!o} .

 If the part  l ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 l_{\ell(K)-lo(K)+1}  lies to the left of the part  l f ​ o l_{f\!o} , then move  k 1 k_{1}  into  L L  between the parts  l ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 l_{\ell(K)-lo(K)+1}  and  l ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 2 l_{\ell(K)-lo(K)+2} .

 (2)  
 
 The map  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  is injective. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 3 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{3} .
Recall that 
 

 
 
 𝐓 3 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , f ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) < 0 , k 1  is odd } . \mathbf{T}_{3}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,f(j_{1},k_{1},2)<0,\,\text{$k_{1}$ is odd}\}. 
 
 
 Let  I = φ 3 ​ ( J , K , L ) = J ​ Q ​ R I=\varphi_{3}(J,K,L)=JQR , where  Q Q  and  R R  are defined by
 Eq.   3.24 . Let us check  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} .
In fact, we have
 Θ I ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) = 1 \Theta_{I}(2m+1)=1 ,
since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 2 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+2  and  j − 1 ≥ 2 j_{-1}\geq 2  by definition.
On the other hand,
assume that  Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) = 0 \Theta_{I}(4m+3)=0 .
Since
 \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 4 ​ m + 3 − k 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=4m+3-k_{1} ,
the composition  R R  has a prefix  R ′ R^{\prime}  of odd size   k 1 k_{1} .
Since  R R  has a prefix  l 1 ​ ⋯ ​ l min ⁡ { f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 } ​ k 1 l_{1}\dotsm l_{\min\{f\!(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-lo(K)+1\}}k_{1}  and since the prefix  l 1 ​ ⋯ ​ l min ⁡ { f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 } l_{1}\dotsm l_{\min\{f\!(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-lo(K)+1\}}  is even, we have  min ⁡ { f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 } = 0 \min\{f\!(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-lo(K)+1\}=0 . In fact,  f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 ≥ 1 f\!(L)-1\geq 1  since  l 1 = 2 l_{1}=2  and  ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) + 1 ≥ 1 \ell(K)-lo(K)+1\geq 1  since  ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − l ​ o ​ ( K ) ≥ 0 \ell(K)-lo(K)\geq 0 . This contradiction proves  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} . 
 
 
 Next, we will show that
 I ∈ 𝒮 3 I\in\mathcal{S}_{3} .
Since  l 1 = 2 l_{1}=2 , we find  f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 ≥ 1 f\!(L)-1\geq 1  and  r 1 = l 1 = 2 r_{1}=l_{1}=2 .
Since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | L \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{L}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1  is odd and  k 1 k_{1}  is odd,
the composition  R R  has at least two odd parts.
From definition,
it is direct to see
that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox  This proves  I ∈ 𝒮 3 I\in\mathcal{S}_{3} . 
 
 
 Second, let us show that  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  is injective.
Suppose that  ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 3 (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{3}  and

Let  I = J ​ Q ​ R = J ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ I=JQR=J^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} .
By definition of  φ 3 \varphi_{3} , the composition  J J  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 2 2m+2 ,
and so is  J ′ J^{\prime} .
Thus  J = J ′ J=J^{\prime} 
and
 Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} .
Assume that  Q ≠ Q ′ Q\neq Q^{\prime} . Then  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ≠ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\neq\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}} .

without loss of generality.
Then there exists a composition  M ≠ ∅ M\neq\emptyset  such that

 a contradiction.
Thus  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime} .
As a result, we obtain  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} . In summary, we have 
 

 
 
 ( J , Q , R ) = ( J ′ , Q ′ , R ′ ) . (J,Q,R)=(J^{\prime},Q^{\prime},R^{\prime}). 
 
 
 Since  K K  (resp.,  K ′ K^{\prime} )
is obtained by inserting the first odd part of  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} )
at the beginning of  Q Q  (resp.,  Q ′ Q^{\prime} ),
we find  K = K ′ K=K^{\prime} .
Since  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} )
is obtained from  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ) by removing the first odd part,
we find  L = L ′ L=L^{\prime} .
This proves the injectivity of  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  and completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now let us handle  𝐓 4 \mathbf{T}_{4} . Recall that 
 

 
 
 𝐓 4 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , K ≥ L } . \mathbf{T}_{4}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,K\geq L\}. 
 
 
 We call a composition  odd 
if it has only odd parts.
For  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 4 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{4} ,
let  U = U ⁡ ( K ) U=U(K)  be the composition obtained from  K K  by moving its longest odd suffix to the beginning; in other words, 
 

 
 
 U ⁡ ( K ) = K 2 ​ K 1 , U(K)=K_{2}K_{1}, 
 
 
 where  K 2 K_{2}  is the longest odd suffix of  K K , and  K 1 K_{1}  is the prefix of  K K  such that
 K = K 1 ​ K 2 K=K_{1}K_{2} . Then  u − 1 u_{-1}  is even, i.e., 
 

 
 (3.26) 
 
 ℓ ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( K ) ) − l ​ o ​ ( U ⁡ ( K ) ) ≥ 1 . \ell(U(K))-lo(U(K))\geq 1. 
 
 
 Note that  K 2 K_{2}  is empty if  k − 1 k_{-1}  is even; in that case  U ⁡ ( K ) = K U(K)=K .
For any  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 4 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{4} , let  I = J ​ U ​ ( K ) ​ L I=JU(K)L . 
 
 
 Define  φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)  to be the composition obtained in the following  3 3  steps: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Insert a bar between the parts  u − 1 u_{-1}  and  l 1 l_{1}  in  I I . 
 
 (2)  
 
 Locate all odd parts of  I I  that are nearest to the bar.
Since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | K \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | L \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{K}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{L}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1 , such a part always exists. When such a part is
not unique, the number of such parts must be two; in that case, we assign the part lying to the right of the bar to be the desired one. 
 
 (3)  
 
 Move this odd part to the symmetric position on the other side of the bar. 
 
 
 For example, 
 

 
 (3.27) 
 
 φ 41 ​ ( 86 , 2335 , 232222 ) \displaystyle\varphi_{41}(86,2335,232222) 
 = 865333222222 , and \displaystyle=865333222222,\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 (3.28) 
 
 φ 42 ​ ( 86 , 22333 , 222232 ) \displaystyle\varphi_{42}(86,22333,222232) 
 = 8633222232232 . \displaystyle=8633222232232. 
 
 
 Since  u − 1 u_{-1}  is even, we can infer that the odd part is not the one
adjacent to the bar; thus 
 

 
 (3.29) 
 
 φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) ≠ J ​ U ​ ( K ) ​ L . \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)\neq JU(K)L. 
 
 
 We will need  Eq.   3.29  in proving  φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝒜 \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)\in\mathcal{A} ; this is also the
reason for why we introduce the operation  U U . 
 
 
 For technical convenience, we give an algebraic definition for  φ 4 \varphi_{4} , which is equivalent to the combinatorial one above.
Let 
 

 
 
 𝐓 4 ′ = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , k 1 = l 1 = 2 } . \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,k_{1}=l_{1}=2\}. 
 
 
 Then  𝐓 4 ⊂ 𝐓 4 ′ \mathbf{T}_{4}\subset\mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime} , and  𝐓 4 ′ \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}  as
 𝐓 4 ′ = 𝐓 41 ⊔ 𝐓 42 \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}_{41}\sqcup\mathbf{T}_{42} , where 
 

 
 
 𝐓 41 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{41} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , f ( L ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ( U ( K ) ) − l o ( U ( K ) ) } , and \displaystyle=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,f\!(L)-1\leq\ell(U(K))-lo(U(K))\},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 𝐓 42 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{42} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , f ( L ) − 1 > ℓ ( U ( K ) ) − l o ( U ( K ) ) } . \displaystyle=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,f\!(L)-1>\ell(U(K))-lo(U(K))\}. 
 
 
 For any  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 41 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{41} ,
define the map  φ 41 : 𝐓 41 → 𝒜 \varphi_{41}\colon\mathbf{T}_{41}\to\mathcal{A}  by 
 

 
 (3.30) 
 
 φ 41 ​ ( J , K , L ) = J ​ Q ​ R , \varphi_{41}(J,K,L)=JQR, 
 
 
 which is obtained from the composition  J ​ U ​ ( K ) ​ L JU(K)L  by moving the first odd part  l f ​ o l_{f\!o}  of  L L  into  U ⁡ ( K ) U(K)  such that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | − l ​ o ​ ( Q ) = f ⁡ ( L ) − 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}-lo(Q)=f\!(L)-1 , i.e.,

 a contradiction.

Since  Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} , we deduce that  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime} .
It follows that  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} .
In summary, we have 
 

 
 
 ( P , Q , R ) = ( P ′ , Q ′ , R ′ ) . (P,Q,R)=(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime},R^{\prime}). 
 
 
 Since  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 42 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{42} ,
we have  f ⁡ ( R ′ ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ′ ) f\!(R^{\prime})-1\leq\ell(Q^{\prime})-lo(Q^{\prime}) .
Hence  f ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ) f\!(R)-1\leq\ell(Q)-lo(Q) .
Since  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3} , we find
  r f ⁡ ( R ) + 1 r_{f\!(R)+1}  is odd.
Thus  r f ⁡ ( R ′ ) + 1 ′ r_{f\!(R^{\prime})+1}^{\prime}  is odd,
contradicting  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 42 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{42} .
This proves  𝒮 3 ∩ 𝒮 42 = ∅ \mathcal{S}_{3}\cap\mathcal{S}_{42}=\emptyset . 
 
 (4)  
 
 Assume that

 P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} ,

 ( \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ) \displaystyle(\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}) 
 = ( 2 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m + 1 − r f ​ o ,  2 ​ m + 1 + r f ​ o ) , and \displaystyle=(2m+2,\,2m+1-r_{f\!o},\,2m+1+r_{f\!o}),\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 ( \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ) \displaystyle(\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}) 
 = ( 2 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m + 1 + q l ​ o ′ ,  2 ​ m + 1 − q l ​ o ′ ) . \displaystyle=(2m+2,\,2m+1+q^{\prime}_{lo},\,2m+1-q^{\prime}_{lo}). 
 
 
 As before, we have  P = P ′ P=P^{\prime} ,  Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} , and we can write 
 

 
 
 I = P Q M R ′ , where  Q ​ M = Q ′ ,  M ​ R ′ = R , and  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r f ​ o + q l ​ o ′ . I=PQM\!R^{\prime},\quad\text{where
$QM=Q^{\prime}$,
$M\!R^{\prime}=R$, and
$\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{f\!o}+q^{\prime}_{lo}$.} 
 
 
 We proceed according to the number of odd parts of  M M . 
 
 
 Suppose that  M M  has no odd parts.
Since  Q ​ M = Q ′ QM=Q^{\prime} ,
we have  ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ) + 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ′ ) \ell(Q)-lo(Q)+1\leq\ell(Q^{\prime})-lo(Q^{\prime}) ;
since  R = M ​ R ′ R=M\!R^{\prime} ,
we have  f ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≥ f ⁡ ( R ′ ) f\!(R)-1\geq f\!(R^{\prime}) ;
since  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 41 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{41} ,
we have
 f ⁡ ( R ′ ) − 1 ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ′ ) f\!(R^{\prime})-1\geq\ell(Q^{\prime})-lo(Q^{\prime}) . Therefore, 
 

 
 (3.32) 
 
 f ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≥ f ⁡ ( R ′ ) ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ′ ) + 1 ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − l ​ o ​ ( Q ) + 2 , f\!(R)-1\geq f\!(R^{\prime})\geq\ell(Q^{\prime})-lo(Q^{\prime})+1\geq\ell(Q)-lo(Q)+2, 
 
 
 which is impossible since  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} . 
 
 
 Otherwise,  M M  has at least two odd parts
since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r f ​ o + q l ​ o ′ \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{f\!o}+q^{\prime}_{lo}  is even.
As a consequence,  f ⁡ ( M ) − 1 + ℓ ⁡ ( M ) − l ​ o ​ ( M ) ≠ n − 1 f\!(M)-1+\ell(M)-lo(M)\neq n-1 .
Since  Q ′ = Q ​ M Q^{\prime}=QM , we have  q l ​ o ′ = m l ​ o q_{lo}^{\prime}=m_{lo} ;
since  R = M ​ R ′ R=MR^{\prime} , we have  r f ​ o = m f ​ o r_{f\!o}=m_{f\!o} .
Therefore, the composition  M M  contains two parts: one is of value  r f ​ o r_{f\!o} , the other is of value  q l ​ o ′ q_{lo}^{\prime} .
Together with  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r f ​ o + q l ​ o ′ \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{f\!o}+q_{lo}^{\prime} ,
we derive that  M = r f ​ o ​ q l ​ o ′ M=r_{f\!o}\,q_{lo}^{\prime} .
Since  R = M ​ R ′ R=MR^{\prime} , we obtain  r 1 = r f ​ o r_{1}=r_{f\!o}  is odd.
Since  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} , we have  r 1 = 2 r_{1}=2 , a contradiction.
This proves  ( S 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 ) ∩ 𝒮 41 = ∅ (S_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42})\cap\mathcal{S}_{41}=\emptyset . 
 
 
 In summary, the sets
 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} ,
 𝒮 3 \mathcal{S}_{3} ,
 𝒮 41 \mathcal{S}_{41} 
and  𝒮 42 \mathcal{S}_{42} 
are pairwise disjoint.
This completes the proof.
∎
